How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited. No reminder is given when the paper already cites that replication or reproduction (matched by its DOI).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI: the DOI in the paper’s reference list, or one looked up with add_bib_match() before the module runs (see ref_table()). References without a DOI are not checked.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

In experience-based risky choice, acquiring and integrating information about the available options is costly in terms of cognitive resources and time. A central question is therefore how decision makers allocate their time to achieve sufficiently high decision quality without incurring excessive time costs. Recent theories formalize this tradeoff and distinguish between two choice environments: When the utility difference between two options is known, and only the identity of the better option has to be identified, evidence accumulation with a fixed decision threshold, that is, a fixed decision quality, is optimal. In contrast, when the utility difference is unknown, a collapsing threshold is optimal, implying that the targeted decision quality is being reduced over time. Here, we investigate whether people adapt their decision thresholds to these different conditions when choosing between two lotteries whose outcomes were presented as rapidly changing dot clouds. Both behavioral and cognitive modeling results indicate that participants did not systematically adjust their decision strategy; across both the known and unknown utility difference conditions, most participants' data were explained better by a collapsing threshold model. Hence, decision makers do not optimally account for initial information about the utility difference. We discuss possible explanations for this, including epistemic uncertainty, task unfamiliarity, and increasing time costs. We conclude that variability in risky choice can be understood as arising, in part, from the tradeoff between the benefits and costs of evidence accumulation, and that cognitive models should incorporate this tradeoff to better characterize rational behavior.

Do People Use Prior Information to Adopt an Optimal Speed-Accuracy Tradeoff in Experience-Based Risky Decisions?

Risky decisions are a ubiquitous part of everyday life, from relatively simple ones, such as choosing a new restaurant for lunch, to more complex and consequential ones, such as selecting a stock for investment. In many such situations, people are unaware of the utility differences among the available options, such as when an investor chooses between two new investment opportunities. In these cases, people need to learn the utility of each option and then make a decision. In contrast, under some circumstances, the decision maker might be aware of the utility difference between the options, but not which option has the higher utility. For example, a frequent traveler choosing between two security queues may know how quickly each of the two security officers works but not which officer is serving which queue. In this scenario, the utility difference between the two queues is known to the decision maker apriori. However, at the time of queue selection, they still need to find out which queue is faster. The aim of this paper is to empirically investigate how prior knowledge about the utility difference between two available options can shape the decision process of risky choices.

To describe decisions under risk, expected utility theory and its variants describe behavior as decision makers maximizing their subjective utility (Kahneman & Tversky, 1979; Luce, 1959; Tversky & Kahneman, 1992). However, these models ignore the fact that processing information and finding the option with the highest subjective utility is costly in terms of cognitive effort and processing time (Bhui et al., 2021; Lieder & Griffiths, 2020; Schulze et al., 2025). Hence, there is a tradeoff between maximizing the subjective utility and the time spent on a given decision problem. This can be observed empirically in many tasks as a speed-accuracy tradeoff (e.g., Fiedler et al., 2021; Heitz, 2014; Pachella & Pew, 1968). Studies on this tradeoff have found that when people take longer time on a choice problem, they choose the best option more frequently -they have a higher decision quality (e.g., Kvam & Busemeyer, 2020; Miletić et al., 2021; Olschewski & Rieskamp, 2021; Wu et al., 2022). 1 Although longer deliberation might increase the probability of selecting the best option, accounting for the cost of deliberation time, longer deliberation might not always be beneficial. For example, when the two available options are very similar in their subjective utility, longer deliberation does not yield better overall outcomes once the costs of information processing are taken into account. Therefore, achieving an optimal tradeoff between deliberation time and picking a good option is crucial (e.g., Bhui, 2019a; Marshall et al., 2022; Pirrone et al., 2022).

Evidence accumulation models (EAM) provide a theoretical framework for studying the interactions between decision time and decision quality (Busemeyer et al., 2019; Hadian Rasanan, Evans, Fontanesi, et al., 2024; Heathcote & Matzke, 2022; Ratcliff et al., 2016).

Historically, accounting for response time in decision models can be traced back to early work by Stone (1960), who presented the core idea of modeling the evidence accumulation process using random walk models (see also, Laming, 1968; Link & Heath, 1975). 2 EAMs assume that decision makers sequentially accumulate noisy evidence until the evidence supporting one option reaches a decision threshold. Based on this assumption, researchers have developed numerous EAMs to investigate how response times and decision quality are related in risky and preferential choice (e.g., Baldassi et al., 2020; Busemeyer, 1985; Busemeyer & Townsend, 1993; Diederich & Trueblood, 2018; Gluth et al., 2020; Hadian Rasanan et al., 2026; Krajbich et al., 2010; Trueblood et al., 2014). These models describe the dynamics of deliberation across a range of settings, including binary choice (e.g., Busemeyer & Townsend, 1993; Krajbich et al., 2010), multi-alternative and multi-attribute choice (e.g., Gluth et al., 2020; Roe et al., 2001; Trueblood et al., 2014), and pricing judgments (e.g., Hadian Rasanan et al., 2026; Kvam & Busemeyer, 2020). By jointly accounting for choices and response times, EAMs have documented systematic relationships between decision speed and decision quality and explained how these relationships emerge from the underlying dynamics of evidence accumulation (e.g., Diederich, 2003; Kvam & Busemeyer, 2020; Shevlin et al., 2022).

One successful specification of EAMs in explaining both preferential and inferential choices is the diffusion decision model (DDM; Ratcliff, 1978; Ratcliff & McKoon, 2008; Ratcliff & Rouder, 1998; Ratcliff et al., 2016), which formalizes the latent process of evidence accumulation as a Wiener process. The model posits that decision makers accumulate noisy evidence for one option relative to the other at a constant mean rate -referred to as the drift rate -until the accumulated relative evidence in favor of one option exceeds a fixed decision threshold. In this framework, the decision threshold can be interpreted as the amount of evidence required to make a decision and thus as the target decision quality. Recently, Webb (2019) showed that the DDM can be considered the process model underlying the random utility models traditionally used to study risky and preferential choices.

An important property of the DDM is that it provides the optimal solution for balancing the costs and benefits of evidence accumulation. In situations where the decision maker knows the utilities of the two available options and knows that they do not change across trials, but still has to figure out which is the option with the higher utility, the sequential probability ratio test provides the optimal solution (Bogacz et al., 2006; Moran, 2015; Wald & Wolfowitz, 1948). Because the probability ratio test provides the statistical foundation of the DDM (Gold & Shadlen, 2007), this model is optimal when the utility difference is known and fixed across trials. In other words, it achieves a desired level of decision quality with the minimum mean response time or, equivalently, maximizes decision quality for a fixed mean response time. 3Building on this framework, Fudenberg et al. (2018) formalized the effect of prior knowledge on the optimal evidence accumulation policy. Fudenberg et al. (2018) assumed that evidence accumulation is costly and the final stopping time determines the total cost, which is deducted from the final reward. Under these assumptions, the optimal evidence accumulation process is a diffusion process in which the drift rate is defined as the difference between the options' expected utilities. The optimal form of the threshold then depends on the decision environment: When decision makers know the utility difference of the options (but not which option has the higher utility), the optimal timing of the decision is obtained by the fixed decision threshold DDM as described in the previous paragraph. In contrast, when they do not know the utility difference between the options in advance, the optimal decision threshold declines hyperbolically as a function of time (i.e., approaches zero as time passes). This result aligns with previous theoretical findings showing that when there is heterogeneity in the difficulty of choice problems, that is, there is uncertainty about the underlying utility difference between two options, a DDM with collapsing decision thresholds is optimal (e.g., Drugowitsch et al., 2012; Tajima et al., 2016).

Within the DDM, a collapsing decision threshold implies that the decision maker requires less evidence to choose among options as time passes. This can be optimal because, as more costly deliberation time elapses without a decision being reached, the more likely it is that the two options are similar in utility and thus choosing the option with (slightly) higher utility provides little additional utility. 4 The intuitive implication of Fudenberg et al. (2018) theory is that when the decision maker knows the utility difference in advance, the difficulty level of the choice problem is known, and the decision maker can set the decision threshold to achieve the desired level of 4 The origin of the evidence accumulation models with time-varying decision threshold traces back to early work by Ashby (1983). Later, neuroscience studies (Churchland et al., 2008; Ditterich, 2006) reintroduced the idea of time-varying decision threshold models by formalizing the collapsing-threshold DDM. They found that the collapsing-threshold DDM is more consistent with the neural signature of the evidence accumulation process. Since then, several studies have compared collapsing thresholds with fixed-threshold models (e.g., Hadian Rasanan, Schumacher, et al., 2025; Hawkins et al., 2015; Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Voskuilen et al., 2016; Zhang et al., 2014). So far, empirical tests of the descriptive accuracy of collapsing threshold models have mostly focused on perceptual (e.g., Evans & Hawkins, 2019; Evans, Hawkins, & Brown, 2020; Hawkins et al., 2015) or epistemic decision tasks with an objectively correct response (Bhui, 2019b; Gluth et al., 2012; Khodadadi et al., 2017). These studies generally found evidence favoring collapsing thresholds to fit the data better than a constant threshold (e.g., Khodadadi et al., 2017; Malhotra et al., 2017). accuracy in a minimum time. However, in the unknown utility difference setting, the decision maker does not know the difficulty of the choice problem in advance and can only learn about it over the sampling process. Therefore, they cannot determine the optimal stopping point before starting the evidence accumulation process. In this case, the fixed decision threshold is not optimal because if the options were equally good (or the difference was small), then a long deliberation time to reach the fixed decision threshold is not beneficial because the cost of evidence accumulation is greater than the utility difference that the decision maker can gain from choosing the best option. Alternatively, if one option was much better than the other one (large utility difference), the decision maker can learn this very quickly and, with a high drift rate, still reach the decision threshold before the threshold collapses significantly.

Therefore, a rational decision maker with finite cognitive resources and time costs should adjust the decision threshold to reduce the target decision quality, terminate deliberation time, and move on to the next rewarding task. Going back to the example of a visitor who needs to choose between two new restaurants, the decision maker might decide to just pick one of the two restaurants after deliberating for some time, with the advantage of freeing time to think about other things and with the disadvantage that their choice might not be optimal (i.e., the food quality not being best). In contrast to standard random utility models (Luce, 1959; Train, 2009), which capture the variability in choice behavior through unexplained noise in the utility representation, the current theoretical framework explains it as a voluntary solution to tradeoff the costs of deliberation time and the benefits of an optimal choice.

In this paper, we empirically investigate how adaptively decision makers adjust their decision thresholds across different risky-choice environments. To this end, we follow the theoretical analysis of Fudenberg et al. (2018) and contrast choice environments with known and unknown utility differences. In general, risky choices have been studied either by describing probability distributions to participants or by presenting individual outcomes from an underlying distribution (see Hertwig et al., 2004; Wulff et al., 2018, for the differences between these two paradigms). So far, the only study directly contrasting known versus unknown utility difference conditions and their implications for decision thresholds examined description-based risky choice (Olschewski et al., 2025). However, in many situations, decision makers are not provided with a description of the full outcome distribution, but instead can experience samples of noisy evidence (Olschewski et al., 2024). For example, traders may use recent price movements to infer market direction and decide whether to buy a stock. Therefore, in this paper, we study experience-based risky decisions using a fast sampling paradigm that fits both real-world decision processes as well as the theoretical framework of Fudenberg et al. (2018). Our experimental task is based on the flash gambling task (Zeigenfuse et al., 2014), in which participants automatically see a sequence of dot clouds in rapid succession. This paradigm has been used in several studies to examine cognitive processes in preferential decision making (Pleskac et al., 2019 (Pleskac et al., , 2023)). Crucially, this paradigm allows participants to be informed upfront about the expected utility difference between the two options without making the task trivial.

Methods

We conducted an experiment to investigate whether prior knowledge about the available choice options influences decision-making strategies. Specifically, we examined whether knowing the utility difference between the two available options affects the dynamics of the decision threshold in experience-based risky decisions.

Materials

Participants were asked to choose between two clouds of flashing dots; the flash gambling task (Zeigenfuse et al., 2014). In this task, each dot corresponds to a fixed monetary reward, and the number of dots in each cloud was repeatedly updated every fifty milliseconds.

Participants could observe the samples for as long as they wanted and were asked to select their preferred option, assuming that the number of dots on the next draw/flash would determine their monetary reward. We created four types of dot clouds, which varied in the number of dots underlying the distributions. In particular, they were normal distributions with two levels of expected value (40 or 50) and two levels of standard deviation (2 or 12).

At each frame, to show a random draw of dots on the screen, we sampled a random number from the underlying normal distribution, and then rounded the generated number to its nearest integer to derive the number of dots to be shown on the frame. To avoid extreme outliers, we truncated the distributions at three standard deviations from the mean. With these four types of dot clouds, we designed three choice problems summarized in Table 1. These three choice problems were selected from all combinations of expected values and standard deviation to systematically manipulate the difficulty of the choice problems and elicit the risk preferences of participants. In these choice problems, the higher expected value (EV) option is either riskier (choice problem 1), or safer(choice problem 2), or both options have the same expected value, with one riskier and one safer option. For instance, in choice problem 1, a (sufficiently) risk-averse participant could select the option with a lower expected value in order to avoid the risk associated with the increased variance of the higher-EV option. The choice problems were designed so that the range of outcomes for the safer option overlaps with that of the riskier option. The EVs and standard deviations (SDs) were selected following a pilot study to design engaging choice options.

Procedure

Before starting the main task, participants went through detailed and interactive instructions, which included a 10-second presentation of samples of all four types of dot clouds, practice rounds, and the comprehension question. 5 Additionally, participants were 5 The comprehension question was designed to make sure that participants learned the task and paid enough attention to the instructions. The question was "What is your task in this study?" with the possible answers informed that we randomly selected one trial for a real monetary bonus. For this trial, we would draw a random sample from the option chosen by the participant and pay a bonus proportional to the number of dots in the sample. The exchange rate was one dot equal to British £0.02. So, based on their decision, participants could receive a bonus of up to £1.72.

In the main task, participants completed six experimental blocks (three blocks for each condition), each containing 60 trials. The main manipulation to test whether people adapt their decision threshold across choice environments was implemented as two types of blocks: those with known and those with unknown utility differences. Both types of blocks were presented three times in a randomized order. Hence, participants completed 180 trials per condition (i.e., known vs. unknown utility difference).

At the beginning of the known utility difference blocks, we informed participants that they would only encounter one type of choice problem during that block and that only the position of the two options was shuffled after each trial and could thus differ in each trial. To learn which choice problem the participants encountered in a given block, they were presented with random draws of two dot clouds from the selected choice problem for ten seconds. Therefore, they learned the distribution of dot clouds of each option and consequently the utility difference between the two options, and they were informed that they would only encounter this choice problem (fixed utility difference) during this block (with just shuffling the positions of each option). These two features (i.e., participants learned the dot cloud distributions of both available options and knew that the options were the same in every trial in the respective block) satisfy the essential theoretical condition of the known utility difference environment defined by Fudenberg et al. (2018). To make the statistical procedure in each block as salient as possible, we included a shuffling animation before each trial for three seconds. In the known utility difference blocks, the shuffling a) "Choose the circle with the fewest dots", b) "Choose the circle that you prefer to receive an additional flash of dots from", c) "Choose the circle on the right side of the screen", and d) "Choose one circle as quickly as possible when you see a dot". If participants did not answer the comprehension question correctly, they could not continue with the experiment, and we compensated them only for the time spent on instructions.

animation contained only the two circles from either side of the screen being shaken behind a rectangular shape in the middle, and then randomly placed back on either side, depicting that the choice problem does not change during the block (see Figure 1, upper row).

In the unknown utility difference blocks, we informed participants that the choice problem could vary on each trial and could include any of the four options presented previously, in a random pairing. We presented each of the three created choice problems exactly twenty times in random order. Again, in each trial, the position of the two dot clouds (left or right) was randomized. In the unknown utility difference blocks, the shuffling animation contained four circles -two coming into the center from the positions they occupy on screen during choice, and two coming into the center from off screen -showing that the upcoming choice problem could contain different options than the previous one. After the shuffling animation, only two circles remained on the screen, while the other two moved off the screen, and the sampling phase started (see Figure 1 bottom row).

The full procedure of a single trial in the known and unknown conditions is presented in Figure 1. Each trial starts with a 3-second shuffling animation (which differs between known and unknown conditions). After that, when the animation finishes, the two circles remain on the screen, and the sampling phase starts automatically. Every 50 milliseconds, a new sample from each dot cloud appears on the screen, and this process continues until the participants respond by pressing 'F' for the left option or 'J' for the right option. After responding, depending on the response time, if the response time was longer than 250 ms and shorter than 12000 ms, feedback on the selected option was given for 1 second by presenting a green frame around the selected option. If the response time was shorter than 250 ms, then a "Too Fast" feedback was presented to participants. Similarly, if the response time was longer than 12000 ms, a "Too Slow" feedback was presented to participants. It is worth noting that the "Too Slow" feedback was given only after the participant responded, and we did not interrupt the sampling procedure. So, they could see as many samples as they wanted during the sampling phase. After the feedback, the next trial started automatically by presenting the shuffling animation. At the end of the experiment, the randomly selected choice problem, the participant's choice, as well as the gained random sample and its corresponding bonus payment in British pounds, were revealed. The bonus payment was paid out to participants after the end of the experiment.

Participants

The experiment was approved by the Ethics Committee of the University of Basel (submission ID: 035-23-1). The experimental methods, the sample size, all exclusion criteria, the computational modeling approach, and all analyses -unless otherwise stated in the Results section -were preregistered prior to data collection on OSF (https://osf.io/23yqe/).

To detect group mean differences of at least Cohen's d = 0.28 in choices and response times between our two block types using a paired t-test, we aimed to recruit at least 108 participants. To account for up to 10% exclusions due to preregistered exclusion criteria, we recruited 120 participants via Prolific, an online crowdworking platform. Data from four participants were not stored properly on the server due to some technical issues. So, we removed those four participants from the study. We were left with 116 participants (Male = 71 and Female = 45) with ages ranging from 18 to 50 (mean = 33.00 and standard deviation = 8.18). We compensated their participation with £6, and the median completion time was 57 minutes. Additionally, participants obtained an average bonus of £0.91 (range = [£0.44, £1.46]).

For robust analyses, we excluded trials with outlier response times that were too fast (RT < 250 ms) or too slow (RT > 12, 000 ms). We further excluded participant-specific outliers for each of the six blocks (defined as beyond participants' median ±3 × SD in a given block). Applying these exclusion criteria removed 1249 trials, or 3% of all trials, from all 116 remaining participants. We preregistered to exclude participants with more than 25% outlier trials. However, no subject was removed from the study due to too much outlier data.

Computational models

We fitted five variants of the DDM to the choices and response times in our experiment. Two models incorporate a fixed decision threshold, while the other three models have collapsing decision thresholds. The first model (FT-DDM) does not include any across-trial variability and assumes that the drift rate (v) remains constant across trials. Based on Fudenberg et al. (2018), this model is an optimal solution for the known utility difference condition. The second model (FT-DDM-DV) includes across-trial variability in the drift rates (η). This model assumes that the drift rate varies from trial to trial and is normally distributed with mean v and standard deviation η. Although this model is not consistent et al. (2018) showed that the optimal threshold must nonlinearly converge 6 There is a long debate on comparing the collapsing threshold models and fixed threshold models with drift rate variability (e.g., Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Voskuilen et al., 2016).

to zero as time approaches infinity. Based on this essential condition and numerical tests, they showed that the hyperbolic collapsing threshold best approximates the optimal decision threshold. Therefore, we considered the hyperbolic collapsing threshold (HCT-DDM) with no variability in drift rate as the third model. However, other alternatives, such as the exponential function, also satisfy the essential optimality condition (i.e., converging to zero at time infinity). Therefore, we also tested the exponential collapsing threshold (ECT-DDM) as the fourth model to assess the robustness of our main conclusions.

Finally, we considered the linear collapsing threshold (LCT-DDM). Although this function does not satisfy the essential optimality condition, a linear formulation is included due to its good parameter recovery (Evans, Trueblood, & Holmes, 2020; Hadian Rasanan, Schumacher, et al., 2025), enabling us to reliably measure the decay rate value, and it approximates the behavior of non-linear collapsing threshold models (Katsimpokis et al., 2020).

In all of these models, we implemented a separate free parameter v i for the drift rate of each choice problem i. In contrast, the parameters for the threshold dynamics (i.e., starting threshold θ and decay rate λ) and non-decision time (τ ) were held constant across choice problems. Also, we did not include starting point biases in the models, so that the accumulator always started from the middle (i.e., x0 = 0). For choice problems 1 and 2, we coded the option with the higher EV as corresponding to the upper threshold. For choice problem 3, in which both options had the same EV, we coded the option with higher variance as corresponding to the upper threshold. Figure 2 illustrates how these models differ in threshold dynamics.

Table 2 contains the formalized definition of threshold dynamics and free parameters of each computation model. Table 2 Summary of computational models employed for data analysis.

Model Threshold dynamic

Free parameters # Free parameters

Note. # Free parameters = Number of free parameters for each individual. v i represents the drift rate for choice problem i, η denotes the drift rate variability, τ is the non-decision time, θ is the starting threshold, and λ denotes the decay rate. In the case of FT-DDM and FT-DDM-DV, θ represents the fixed decision threshold.

We fitted the models separately for each condition (known vs. unknown utility difference) and participant. So, for estimating each model, about 180 trials were available.

To estimate the likelihood function of collapsing threshold diffusion models, we employed the integral equation method (Buonocore et al., 1990; Buonocore et al., 1987; Smith, 2000; Smith & Ratcliff, 2022). The main advantage of this method over simulation methods, such as probability density approximation, is the mathematical guarantee that by considering sufficiently small ∆t, the approximated solution converges to the actual value of the likelihood function (Buonocore et al., 1990; Buonocore et al., 1987; Hadian Rasanan, Evans, et al., 2025). This method has already been used in several studies on collapsing threshold diffusion models (e.g., Evans, Hawkins, & Brown, 2020; Hadian Rasanan, Evans, et al., 2025; Hadian Rasanan, Schumacher, et al., 2025; Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Voskuilen et al., 2016). Details of the likelihood estimation procedure are presented in the Appendix A. We employed the maximum likelihood procedure to estimate the parameters of the models, and as the optimization algorithm, we used the differential evolution routine provided by Scipy.

To validate this approach, we conducted model recovery simulation analyses using the exact same stimuli as in a single condition and for a single participant in our experiment. In sum, we were able to recover the data-generating model from the pair of FT-DDM and HCT-DDM with 98.9% accuracy based on a likelihood-ratio test. The details of the model recovery study are described in Appendix B.

Results

Behavioral results

Figure 3 displays choice proportions and response times separately for both conditions. 7 Participants chose the option with the higher expected value in choice problems 1 and 2 significantly more often than chance (80.23%, t(115) = 22.98, p < 0.001 for choice problem 1, and 81.12%, t(115) = 25.27, p < 0.001 for choice problem 2). In choice problem 3, participants chose the option with higher variance less often than chance (48.54%, t(115) = 3.40, p < 0.001), consistent with risk aversion. Comparing the choice proportions for each choice problem across block types (known or unknown utility difference) resulted in no significant differences. 8 Similarly, there was no significant difference in median response times in the known (1.66 s) and unknown utility difference conditions (1.62 s, t(115) = 1.49, p = 0.138).

This means that participants did not differ meaningfully in their average response time, independent of whether they knew the utility difference upfront or not.

Computational modeling results

Since our main focus is to test the optimality of evidence accumulation processing in risky choice within the framework of Fudenberg et al. (2018), we report only the results for HCT-DDM and FT-DDM here. The results of the other computational models are presented in Appendix D. Model predictions for mean response times and choice proportions are depicted in Figure 3. The predictions of the HCT-DDM closely align with empirical data in both known and unknown utility-difference conditions. However, the FT-DDM overestimated the mean response time for choice problem 3 in both conditions. This indicates that participants adjust their decision threshold to cut off long deliberation times, particularly in choice problem 3, which was much closer in choice proportions to 50-50 than the other choice problems.

1 2 3 Choice problem 1.6 1.8 2.0 2.2 2.4 Mean response time (s) 1 2 3 Choice problem 0.45 0.50 0.55 0.60 0.65 0.70 0.75 0.80 0.85 Mean choice proportion Data and predictions for the known and unknown utility conditions HCT-DDM Known Utility FT-DDM Unknown Utility Data To examine the distribution of response times more closely, in Figure 4, we displayed the mean choice proportions for five response time quantiles. In particular, under collapsing thresholds, we would expect more noise, that is, less extreme choice proportions for the favorite option, in later than in earlier response time quantiles. This is indeed what can be observed in both conditions, though the effect is only visible for the choice problems 1 and 2, and less so for choice problem 3, which is closer to 50-50. HCT-DDM can produce this downward slope in choice proportions toward 50-50 as the response time elapses. In contrast, the FT-DDM cannot produce such a slope and thus overestimates the choice proportions for the favorite option in the fifth response time quantile, particularly in the known condition for choice problems 1 and 2. Moreover, FT-DDM overestimates the fifth response time quantiles in both conditions, while the quantile predictions of the HCT-DDM are more aligned with observed response time quantiles across conditions. In sum, the qualitative model predictions suggest that HCT-DDM better accounts for the behavioral data in both conditions.

Figure 4

Illustration of choice proportion trend as a function of response time. The left panels exhibit empirical data and models' predictions for the known utility difference condition. The right panels are the same plots for the unknown utility difference condition. On each panel, the x-axis represents the response time quantile (i.e., 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, and 0.9), and the yaxis represents the mean choice proportion. The error bars show the 95% participant-level confidence intervals for both simulated and empirical data.

To better distinguish between the models, we now turn to quantitative model comparisons based on the likelihood ratio test that accounts for model complexity. At the group level, HCT-DDM had significantly better fits than FT-DDM in both known (χ 2 (115) = 1926.86, p < 0.001) and unknown (χ 2 (115) = 2019.06, p < 0.001) utility difference conditions. These results were also supported by Bayesian information criteria (BIC; see Table 3). Next, we consider individual-level model comparisons to examine whether the model that won at the group level was also the best-fitting model for the majority of participants.

Based on the individual-participant likelihood ratio test, HCT-DDM fit the data significantly better than FT-DDM for 84 of 116 participants (72.4%) in the known utility difference condition and 80 (68.9%) in the unknown utility difference condition. 9 These model comparisons indicate that participants are predominantly explained better by the collapsing threshold models, both in conditions where it was optimal (i.e., unknown utility difference) and in conditions where it was not optimal (i.e., known utility difference). Moreover, for 64 participants (55.1%), HCT-DDM had a better fit relative to FT-DDM in both known and unknown utility difference conditions. Thus, more than half of the participants employed a consistent collapsing-threshold strategy in both conditions. In contrast, 16 participants (13.7%) were consistently not better explained by a collapsing threshold model in both known and unknown conditions. Accordingly, for around 69% of participants, we did not find evidence for any adoption of their decision threshold across conditions. Figure 5 presents the estimated hyperbolic thresholds for each participant in both conditions (see also Appendix D for linear and exponential thresholds). The illustrated threshold dynamics (upper panels in Figure 5) suggest that participants whose data are better explained by the HCT-DDM show meaningful threshold decay across the observed response times, whereas participants who have a better fit by FT-DDM show a decay rate very close to zero. This is also pronounced in the lower panels of Figure 5, where we presented the joint distribution of estimated values for starting threshold and decay rate. 10 9 The results for ECT-DDM and LCT-DDM are reported in Appendix D. 10 To assess the robustness of our results to additional learning within each block, we removed the first 10 trials from each block and then compared the fitting of FT-DDM versus HCT-DDM, which was the main analysis in this study.

The modeling comparison results showed that the collapsing threshold model explains 75 subjects (64% of the subjects) in the known condition and 76 subjects (65% of the subjects) in the unknown condition better. This implies that our main results are robust to any residual learning that may have occurred during the first 10 trials. Table 3 also reports the mean estimated parameters of all considered cognitive models. In all models, the drift rates for the choice problems 1 and 2, v 1 and v 2 , were significantly positive in both the known and unknown utility difference conditions, indicating that participants preferred the options with higher EV. Additionally, the drift rate for choice problem 3 (v 3 ) had a small negative value, indicating that participants were more willing to select the option with lower variance (i.e., they were risk-averse). Non-decision time τ did not differ across conditions. Thus, prior information about the choice problem seemed not to affect the encoding and motor execution time. 11 Moreover, in both conditions, the decay rate (λ) was relatively large, indicating that, on average, people mostly used collapsing thresholds, even when this was not optimal in the known utility difference condition.

Discussion

In this work, we examined the effect of prior knowledge about the utility difference of the two available options on decision making strategies in experience-based risk taking. Fudenberg et al. (2018) showed that the optimal evidence accumulation strategy in homogeneous-difficulty environments with known utility difference is a fixed threshold DDM.

Whereas the optimal strategy in heterogeneous-difficulty environments with unknown utility differences is approximately a hyperbolic collapsing threshold DDM. In our empirical test of 11 There is a difference between estimated non-decision time across the fixed threshold models and the collapsing threshold models. This could indicate a tradeoff between the considered threshold dynamics and the estimated non-decision time. Such a tradeoff between non-decision time and the threshold dynamics was first reported by Hadian Rasanan, Schumacher, et al. (2025). However, our main non-decision time comparison here was across conditions (known versus unknown). This comparison using the same model family (e.g., estimated τ for FT-DDM across conditions) showed that the non-decision time does not vary across conditions.

adaptive shifts in decision strategies between these two environments, we observed no significant behavioral differences. There was no significant difference in median response times and choice proportions across conditions. At the same time, we observed a trend toward a decrease in choice consistency for the preferred option as time elapses in both conditions, which is consistent with collapsing thresholds. Based on quantitative model comparison analyses, the collapsing-threshold DDMs fitted better than the fixed-threshold DDMs on the aggregate level and for the majority of participants across both known and unknown utility difference conditions. This implies that, on average, people do not utilize prior information on the utility difference between two options to adopt the optimal evidence accumulation strategy. Rather, they always use collapsing decision thresholds, as it would be optimal only when no prior information about the utility difference was available.

Comparing the results of the collapsing threshold diffusion models with the fixed threshold diffusion model that includes across-trial variability in drift rate (FT-DDM-DV) strengthens this conclusion. The FT-DDM-DV has the same number of parameters as the collapsing threshold models and can, in principle, predict the decrease in choice consistency observed in the empirical data.

However, the fits of the collapsing threshold models were better than the FT-DDM-DV, and qualitatively, the latter model overestimated the tail of the response time distribution (for detailed results, see Appendix D).

Our results are similar to previous findings about the persistent use of collapsing thresholds both when the utility difference is known and unknown to the participants in description-based risky choice (Olschewski et al., 2025). We went beyond their results by using a rapid experience-based risk-taking task that arguably covers an important class of real-world decision making and has a strong fit to the assumed decision context in the theoretical framework of Fudenberg et al. (2018). The finding that collapsing-threshold models consistently outperformed the constant-threshold model across description-and experiencebased decisions is particularly noteworthy because these decision formats parallel the distinction between static-and dynamic-stimulus tasks studied in other domains. In static-stimulus tasks, information remains constant throughout evidence accumulation, whereas in dynamicstimulus tasks, information changes over the course of a trial. Previous research suggests that this distinction matters for threshold dynamics: Constant threshold models tend to provide better fits in static-stimuli tasks (e.g., Hawkins et al., 2015; Voskuilen et al., 2016), whereas collapsing threshold models tend to provide better fits in dynamic-stimuli tasks (e.g., Bhui, 2019b; Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Trueblood et al., 2021). In contrast, collapsing thresholds seems to provide a better account of risky choice across both presentation formats, regardless of whether information remains static (decisions from description) or changes during a trial (decisions from experience).

Despite finding no difference between the two decision scenarios, we believe that in our known utility difference condition, we created an experimental situation that made the prior information as reliable, comprehensible, and salient as possible. We did this by communicating the exact reward distributions prior to the choice, providing participants with a large number of samples from the reward distributions, and reminding them before every trial that the options were identical to those in the previous trials through a shuffling animation. Notwithstanding these efforts, we cannot guarantee that all participants perceived our experimental condition as one in which the utility difference between the two options is known with certainty. In particular, epistemic or cognitive uncertainty, that is, uncertainty that stems from internal cognitive rather than external stochastic processes, can affect experience-based (Olschewski & Scheibehenne, 2024) as well as description-based risk taking (Enke & Graeber, 2023). Therefore, even under the current experimental design in the known utility condition, participants might have remained internally uncertain about the utility difference, and, as a result, collapsing thresholds was also optimal in our known utility condition. However, the distinct choice proportions within and across choice problems in our experiment suggest that participants gained a good representation of the values and their preferences in our task.

Another important assumption within the Fudenberg et al. (2018) framework is that the costs of deliberation time are constant. However, during our experimental condition, participants' subjective costs of deliberation time might have increased over time (Drugowitsch et al., 2012; Tajima et al., 2016). When participants do not perceive the costs of deliberation time as constant, the fixed threshold model is no longer optimal for the known utility difference condition. Hence, an alternative explanation for why the collapsing threshold model provided a better fit to our data could be that decision makers in our task might have felt the urge to respond as time passed, which could have increased time costs over time.

Alternatively, in real-world economic contexts, the utility difference between two available options is usually unknown. Rather, the more natural situation is arguably when decision makers have to realize both which option is better and by how much. As a consequence, participants might not be familiar with the strategy of using the utility difference as a cue for a constant threshold for their deliberation. Therefore, they could not make optimal use of it and instead lowered the quality of their decisions over time, as they would in the majority of the choice situations they encountered. As the current study only included six blocks (i.e., three blocks for each condition) in which we did not present any feedback on participants' choices, it might be unlikely that participants adapted to an unfamiliar decision environment (i.e., the known utility difference condition). Studies on practice effects have shown that participants can approach optimal decision thresholds when they receive extensive feedback on their performance in inference tasks (e.g., Evans & Brown, 2017; Evans & Hawkins, 2019; Evans et al., 2019; Khodadadi et al., 2017).

According to these arguments, a potential avenue for future studies is to further examine whether decision makers can learn to switch between collapsing-and fixed-threshold strategies after extensive practice with feedback. Such a study could also clarify whether epistemic uncertainty about the utility difference, unfamiliarity with the choice situation, or increasing time costs are the most likely reasons for the use of collapsing thresholds even in known utility difference situations where it is not optimal. If unfamiliarity is the reason, extensive training should eventually lead to the use of a fixed threshold. In contrast, if epistemic uncertainty about the utility differences or increasing time costs are the reason, the threshold shape is unlikely to change even after extensive training, and the collapsing threshold would still explain the choice behavior better. Also, a study with extensive training allows us to test whether considering separate decision thresholds for each choice problem in the known condition can make a difference in model comparison results.

In this study, we employed the integral equation method to estimate the likelihood function of CT-DDMs (see Appendix A for details). This method has some advantages over other existing methods, such as partial differential equation (e.g., Boehm et al., 2021; Hadian Rasanan, Evans, Rieskamp, & Amani Rad, 2024; Hadian Rasanan et al., 2023; Voss & Voss, 2008), simulation-based (e.g., Turner & Sederberg, 2014), and neural network methods (e.g., Fengler et al., 2021; Radev et al., 2020). It is more reliable because it estimates the exact likelihood value (Buonocore et al., 1990; Buonocore et al., 1987), and it is more efficient because it requires no simulation or pre-training. However, concerning the result that the exponential collapsing threshold model was quantitatively the best among the collapsing threshold models in both conditions (Table 3), we refrain from a strong interpretation. This is because there is high mimicry from all collapsing threshold models for response time and choice patterns, so these differences might not be very reliable (Khodadadi & Townsend, 2015). However, additional source data, like neurophysiological signals, can be helpful (Hadian Rasanan, Schumacher, et al., 2025). For now, we favor the hyperbolic collapsing threshold model because it approximates the optimality solution (Fudenberg et al., 2018) and because of its already mentioned equivalence to linear urgency signal models (Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Trueblood et al., 2021). However, in theory, the optimal threshold does not have to be monotonically decreasing (Fudenberg et al., 2018). Accordingly, other forms of collapsing threshold functions, such as the Weibull function (Hawkins et al., 2015), delayed collapsing, or more generally any continuous (multi-stage) function that asymptotically approaches zero (Diederich & Oswald, 2016), are also cognitively plausible. However, they might not be a good approximation of the optimal strategy as described in Fudenberg et al. (2018). Therefore, an important future research direction is to more reliably distinguish between threshold dynamics.

Our study provides a rigorous, theory-based test of decision-making strategies of humans with finite cognitive resources in different experience-based risky decision environments.

The better fit of collapsing DDMs to our data demonstrates that participants in our task were aware of the tradeoff between the costs and benefits of deliberation time. This means that at least part of the variability in human preferential choice behavior, that is, trials in which participants seemed not to maximize their utility, can be explained by the decline in decision quality in order to save valuable cognitive resources and time. Thus, taking noisy information integration and opportunity costs into account has important implications for the assessment of rational decision making and provides a warning against prematurely dismissing observed behavior as irrational: Decision makers can voluntarily give up the goal to find the best option if they deem that the opportunity costs exceed the deliberation benefits.

Evidence accumulation models with collapsing thresholds can incorporate this tradeoff and are therefore better able to describe the cognitive process of decision making under risk, to predict shifts in choice and response time distributions due to changes in the rewards or costs of a decision scenario, and thus to assess rational behavior under realistic cognitive assumptions.

1.563 (s). Similarly, the average median response time for choice problem 2 in the known condition was 1.699 (s), but in the unknown condition it was 1.558 (s). We did not observe a significant difference in response time across conditions for choice problem 3 (known = 1.793 (s), unknown = 1.799, t(115) = -0.104, p = 0.917).

Appendix A Model fitting using integral equation method

There is no analytical formulation for the likelihood function of diffusion models with timedependent thresholds. Therefore, an approximation method is required to estimate the distributions of the first-passage times of the upper and lower thresholds. Over the past decade, several approximation techniques have been proposed. However, among the existing likelihood estimation techniques, the integral equation method has several advantages, including efficiency, simplicity, and a theoretical foundation that guarantees convergence to the exact likelihood value (Buonocore et al., 1990; Buonocore et al., 1987; Smith, 2000).

The integral equation method has been frequently used in cognitive psychology for various diffusion models (e.g., Hadian Rasanan, Evans, et al., 2025; Smith, 2023; Smith & Ratcliff, 2022; Voskuilen et al., 2016). In this method, the first passage time distributions of the lower and upper thresholds are obtained by approximating the solution of a second-type linear Volterra integral equation as follows where g u (t) and g l (t) denote the first-passage time distributions corresponding to upper and lower thresholds, respectively.

Also, Ψ is the kernel function, which is defined as follows (Giorno et al., 1989; Richter et al., 2023): where f denotes the free transition probability density function and is defined as below:

To approximate the solution of this integral equation, we have used the following discretized scheme, which is proposed by Buonocore et al., 1990. In this scheme, the temporal space is discretized with a time step ∆t = Tmax N , 12 and then the first-passage time densities at the first time step are obtained by:

For the next time steps i = 2, 3, . . . , the following discretized form of the integral equation gives an approximation of the first-passage time distributions:

In practice, T max is considered equal to the longest response time.

gl [b l (i∆t), i∆t|z, 0] = 2Ψ[b l (i∆t), i∆t|z, 0] (A7)

The convergence of this numerical scheme has already been shown (see, Buonocore et al., 1990; Buonocore et al., 1987). In practice, the time step ∆t does not have to be very small to have a good estimation of the first-passage time distribution (Richter et al., 2023).

In this study, we considered ∆t = 0.02 to approximate the first-passage time distributions and estimate the likelihood function of the considered diffusion models.

Appendix B Model recovery

To validate the computational modeling results, we conducted a model recovery study using simulated data. For this purpose, we simulated 1,000 sample parameter sets from the following distributions (i.e., in 500 parameter sets we considered λ = 0 for generating fixed These distributions have been considered due to the mean estimated parameter values reported in Table 3. After that, similar to the employed experimental paradigm, we simulated response time and choice for 180 trials (i.e., 60 trials using each drift rate; corresponding to each choice problem) using a discretized diffusion process as follows, with a time step ∆t = 0.001:

X(0) = 0, and ε ∼ N (0, 1).

We assumed that the decision threshold dynamic in the CT-DDM is a hyperbolic function as considered in the main text (i.e., b u (t) = -b l (t) = θ 1+λt ). We fitted the FT-DDM and the HCT-DDM on each simulated data set and then compared the goodness of fit based on the likelihood ratio test. We have used the same fitting procedure as we employed for the computational modeling of the empirical data. The obtained results showed that we can recover the true generating model with 98.9% accuracy. Based on the high accuracy in model recovery, we can reliably infer the underlying process of evidence accumulation.

The simulation results and the code for the model recovery study are available on OSF (https://osf.io/23yqe/).

Appendix C Additional preregistered behavioral results

As we preregistered, we tested all choice proportions across conditions and choice problems against 50%. In both known and unknown utility difference conditions, participants consistently selected the option with the higher expected value. The choice proportions for choice problems 1 and 2 in the known condition were 80.47% (t(115) = 21.54, p < 0.001) and 81.86% (t(115) = 23.88, p < 0.001), respectively. Also, the choice proportions for choice problems 1 and 2 in the unknown condition were 79.98% (t(115) = 21.95, p < 0.001) and 80.38% (t(115) = 23.06, p < 0.001), respectively. In the known condition, participants chose the option with lower variance significantly more frequently than 50% in the choice problem 3 (51.75%, t(115) = 2.87, and p = 0.004). However, we did not observe a significant deviation from 50% for the choice proportion of the choice problem 3 in the unknown condition (51.17%, t(115) = 1.73, and p = 0.084). Moreover, we checked the difference in the choice proportion of choice problems 1 and 2 across conditions, and we did not observe a significant difference (t(115) = -1.39, p = 0.165 in the known condition, and t(115) = -0.57, p = 0.565 in the unknown condition).

In addition to these registered analyses, we also conducted a post hoc analysis to examine the difference in choice behavior across conditions. Thus, we looked at the difference in choice proportions and median response times across conditions for each choice problem.

For the choice proportions, we did not observe any significant difference between the known and unknown conditions across choice problems (i.e., t(115) = 0.54, p = 0.584 for choice problem 1, t(115) = 1.50, p = 0.135 for choice problem 2, t(115) = -0.60, p = 0.543 for choice problem 3). However, we observed a significant difference in median response time between known and unknown conditions for choice problem 1 (t(115) = 2.89, p = 0.004) and choice problem 2 (t(115) = 2.52, p = 0.012). Participants were faster in the unknown condition in both of these choice problems. Appendix D Additional computational modeling results

This appendix includes the computational modeling results for FT-DDM-DV, ECT-DDM, and LCT-DDM. Figure D1 illustrates the predictions of the FT-DDM-DV, ECT-DDM, and LCT-DDM for mean RT and mean choice proportion across conditions against the empirical data. Similar to HCT-DDM, the predictions of other collapsing threshold diffusion models closely align with the observed mean RT and mean choice proportion. However, FT-DDM-DV overestimates the mean RT in both conditions, in particular for the choice problem 3.

Data and posterior prediction of the computational models for mean response time and choice proportion. The upper panels illustrate data and the prediction for the known utility difference condition, and the lower panels for the unknown utility difference condition.

Usually, the speed-accuracy tradeoff is found in decision problems with many repetitions where learning across trials is minimal, and strategy switches (e.g., from compensatory strategies to non-compensatory heuristics) are rare.

Interested readers are referred to Hadian Rasanan, Evans, Rieskamp, and Amani Rad (2024) for a historical review on sequential sampling models.

This type of optimality is called Wald optimality(Wald & Wolfowitz, 1948). A popular alternative definition of optimality is to maximize the reward rate(Bogacz et al., 2006), which yields the same solution as Wald optimality in environments where both options are equally likely to be better (but see alsoMoran, 2015).

As we cannot directly analyze the latent deliberation time without cognitive modeling, we analyzed response time as an approximation of deliberation time in this part.

We preregistered to test each choice proportion against 50% separately for each block type (known or unknown utility difference). However, as we did not observe any meaningful differences between the conditions, we described the aggregated results here. The pre-registered analyses are reported in the Appendix C.

Comparing FT-DDM with ECT-DDM and LCT-DDM at the group level based on the likelihood ratio test revealed that the collapsing threshold models have significantly better fits compared to the FT-DDM in both known condition (χ 2 (115) = 1735.19, p < 0.001 for ECT-DDM and χ 2 (115) = 1621.37, p < 0.001 for LCT-DDM) and unknown condition (χ 2 (115) = 1850.13, p < 0.001 for ECT-DDM and χ 2 (115) = 1766.92, p < 0.001 for LCT-DDM). The individual model comparison, based on the likelihood ratio test using the ECT-DDM, resulted in a better fit for 83 participants in the known utility difference condition and 80 participants in the unknown utility condition compared to the FT-DDM.

Also, comparing the LCT-DDM with the FT-DDM yielded that 82 participants in the known and 80 participants in the unknown conditions are explained better by the LCT-DDM than the FT-DDM. Figures D3 and D4 present the estimated exponential and linear collapsing thresholds, respectively.
